Compositional Design, Implementation, and Verification of Swarms (Technical Report)
Florian Furbach, Lucas Clorius, Roland Kuhn, Hernán Melgratti, Alceste Scalas, Emilio Tuosto

Abstract
Swarm protocols are a recently introduced formalism for specifying, implementing, and verifying peer-to-peer systems called swarms . A swarm consists of distributed agents called machines that communicate by asynchronous event propagation. Following a local-first model, each machine can progress without requiring continuous connectivity to other machines. Existing models of swarms are not compositional, making the modular development of large and complex swarm applications as well as the reuse of code difficult. We address these issues by presenting novel theory and techniques for the compositional specification, verification, and implementation of swarms. These results enable the correct compositional reuse of pre-existing swarm protocols and machine implementations. We implement these contributions in a companion software artifact which enables the automatic integration of independently designed and verified swarm components.

Introduction

Modern distributed systems face significant challenges in ensuring reliability, availability, and scalability.
Recent work on
swarm protocols

[
41
,
40
]

addresses these challenges by formally specifying the behavior of interacting agents capable of independent yet coordinated progress.
In this approach, a
swarm
is modelled as an ensemble of distributed interacting agents, called
machines
.
Each machine can coordinate with others by emitting
events
(i.e., messages) that propagate asynchronously through the swarm, and by
subscribing
to (some of) the events emitted by other machines. Essentially, swarms are type-based publish/subscribe systems with distributed asynchronous delivery
[
25
]
.
Unlike most publish/subscribe systems that aim for causal-consistent delivery (i.e., machines share a consistent view of the global system state), swarms are designed to operate over inconsistent views, often corresponding to inconsistent cuts of the global state
[
7
]
.
This design choice prioritises availability over consistency
[
29
]
, following a local-first paradigm
[
39
,
38
]
: each machine can make progress independently, without requiring up-to-date global information or a continuously available network connection.
Such an approach enables decentralized decision-making, reducing reliance on central coordination and improving robustness. However, it also introduces potential inconsistencies: machines may take decisions based on stale or divergent state, which may later require reconciliation
[
44
]
.

The approach in

[
41
,
40
]
advocates the
usage of behavioral types
[
35
,
27
,
6
]
to ensure
that a swarm correctly reconciles inconsistencies.
Roughly, the intended global behaviour of the swarm (i.e., the
expected interactions among machines) is formalised as a
swarm
protocol
: a specification that provides a bird’s-eye view of how
machines playing different
roles
should interact at runtime, without causing irreconcilable inconsistencies in the overall state of the swarm.
A swarm protocol can then be
projected
onto the different roles to obtain the corresponding machine specifications.
When an ensemble of machines, each running according to a projection of , is deployed to form a swarm , then (under
well-formedness
conditions discussed later) the swarm is guaranteed to execute in accordance with .

A Use Case of Swarm Protocols and Swarms.

To illustrate the theory and practice of swarm protocols, we present a
simplified use case in factory automation (‘‘Industry
4.0’’).
1
1

1

We borrow the use case of an automatic warehouse
operated by a swarm which has been implemented in the open source
Actyx toolkit
[
4
]
.

The intended global behaviour of the swarm is specified by the

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
protocol illustrated in
Figure

.

\mathtt{T}

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
. A machine playing the forklift role
\mathtt{FL}

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}
. A machine playing role
\mathtt{T}
then picks up the part and emits an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
. Between consecutive requests, the door
\mathtt{D}
may close, emitting an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
.

Figure 4
:
A
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
protocol that can interface with the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
(
Figure

1
) using the role
\mathtt{T}
.

The swarm protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
is then
projected
onto its roles
\mathtt{T}
,
\mathtt{FL}
, and
\mathtt{D}
, yielding a machine specification for each role. For example, the projection of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
onto the role
\mathtt{D}
(door) results in the state machine shown in
Figure

2
.
For each event type, say
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
, a transition labelled
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}!

indicates that the machine
emits
an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
,
while a transition labelled
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}?
indicates that the machine
accepts
an
event of that type.
During the execution of a swarm, when a machine emits an event, the event is appended to the emitting machine’s
local event log
;
then, the event asynchronously propagates to the logs of other machines. A machine changes its state only when it
inspects its log and accepts an event from it;
an event emission does not directly cause a state change
– rather, after an event is emitted, its acceptance triggers a state
transition. For example, in
Figure

2
, state
{0}
of machine
\cdot_{\mathtt{D}}
has a self-loop
emitting an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
, as well as a
transition that accepts the same event type.
The non-atomic nature of event emission (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{\_}}!
) and acceptance (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{\_}}?
) implies that events from different machines may interleave: i.e., after a
machine emits an event, and before that event is accepted, other events from
other machines may be emitted, propagated, and accepted.

In practice, machines can be implemented using the open source Actyx toolkit
[
4
]
, which provides a TypeScript API for defining swarm protocols, implementing machines, and deploying swarms.
Figure

3
shows the TypeScript implementation of the state machine

\cdot_{\mathtt{D}}
in
Figure

2
: the machine is called
door
and plays the role
'
D
'
(line 2).
Lines 5–12 define the states
s0
,
s1
,
s3
that map to states
{0}
,
{1}
, and
{3}
in
\cdot_{\mathtt{D}}
, respectively, as follows.
Line 5 introduces the state named
s0
while lines 6–9 define the transition that emits an event of type
closingTime
, which is declared using the
command
method. The elided code on line 7 implements the business logic associated with the event emission, while line 8 specifies the information attached to the emitted event (in this case, a timestamp).
Lines 11 and 12 introduce the states
s1
and
s3
.
Finally, lines 15–17 define the accepting transitions. In particular, line 16 specifies that when the machine is in state
s0
and receives an event of type
closingTime
, it accepts the event and transitions to state
s3
. The omitted code implements the corresponding application-specific business logic.

Once the code is written, the Actyx toolkit enables static verification to check that a machine’s implementation adheres to an intended behaviour. Specifically, it provides APIs for defining swarm protocols, tools for projecting protocols onto roles, a type extraction tool that reconstructs machines from TypeScript code, and a type-checking tool to check whether the projected and extracted machines are consistent
[
4
]
. For instance, the Actyx toolkit can verify that the code in
Figure

3
conforms to the machine in
Figure

2
, that is projected from
Figure

1
.

Going back to the theory, the use of swarm protocols ensures a key correctness property: if a swarm consists of machines projected onto
the roles of the same swarm protocol (such as
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
in
Figure

1
), then the swarm enjoys
eventual
fidelity
to
[
41
]
. Intuitively, this means that, once every event propagates to every
machine in the swarm , all machines will reach a consensus on which events reflect a transition specified in ,
and how they conform to an execution of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{G}}
. This property also holds if, to increase reliability, the swarm includes multiple replicas of machines projected from the same role (e.g., in the example above, multiple machines may play the forklift role
\mathtt{FL}
). The formulation and proof of eventual fidelity are quite challenging, as they must take into account the shape of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{G}}
, the swarm event propagation mechanics, and the
subscriptions
of each machine (i.e., which events it sees).

Our Objective: Compositional Design and Verification of Swarm Protocols.

The previously-published theory
[
41
]
and implementation
[
4
,
40
]
of swarm protocols only support
monolithic
swarm design and implementation: i.e., they do not support developing a large and complex
swarm by combining modular, reusable swarm protocols and swarms that are designed, implemented, and verified independently.

Consider the (simplified) protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
shown in
Figure

4
where a transport (
\mathtt{T}
)
request
s and picks a part, which is then utilised by an assembly robot
(
\mathtt{A}
) to
build
a car (emitting an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}
). Notably, the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
swarm protocol only specifies
\mathtt{T}
’s events
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
, and does not specify how the part is obtained (i.e., what happens “in between”
\mathtt{T}
’s events); such a detail could be part of another swarm protocol — such as
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
(
Figure

1
), where
\mathtt{T}
gets the part from a forklift
\mathtt{FL}
. Intuitively, the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
protocol is a high-level view of a production process, whereas the composition of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
describes the complete process where both protocols run concurrently with the transport role acting as an interface between them.

Now, suppose that the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
protocol changes into an updated swarm protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory^{\prime}}}
(where, e.g., the assembly robot
\mathtt{A}
coordinates with other factory devices): it would be desirable to compose
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory^{\prime}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
(unchanged), obtaining a complete, updated production process protocol. Correspondingly, the machines projected from the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
swarm protocol should be
reusable:
for example, it should be possible to deploy the implementation of the door role
\mathtt{D}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
(shown in
Figure

3
) into any swarm that is “compatible” with
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
, without requiring manual changes depending on whether other machines implement the protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory^{\prime}}}
.

Unfortunately, this scenario cannot be addressed with swarm protocol results in literature. There is no method to design
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
as separate protocols and compose them at a later stage: a whole
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{FactoryWithWarehouse}}
swarm protocol must be designed at once, and then used to project swarm machines. Moreover, if a detail of the “factory” part of the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{FactoryWithWarehouse}}
swarm protocol changes and leads to an updated protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{FactoryWithWarehouse^{\prime}}}
, the change may also impact the machines of the “warehouse” part — e.g., the machine obtained by projecting
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{FactoryWithWarehouse^{\prime}}}
onto role
\mathtt{D}
may differ from the projection of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{FactoryWithWarehouse}}
onto
\mathtt{D}
in non-trivial ways, meaning that the implementation of the
\mathtt{D}
oor machine in
Figure

3
may not be reusable and must be manually revised. As a consequence, developing new swarms, or adapting existing swarms to new requirements, is a challenging and time-consuming endeavour.
It would be highly desirable to have methods and tools to assemble libraries of swarm protocols and corresponding ready-to-use swarm machines that can be safely and easily composed, reused, and deployed to tackle new scenarios. In this work, we introduce such methods and tools.

Contributions and Outline of the Paper.

We present a novel theory and implementation of compositional swarm design, development, and verification.
Our approach is based on
interfacing roles
:
given
n
swarm protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
with suitable roles acting as interfaces, we define the composition
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
.
Also, given
n
swarms
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{1},\ldots,{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{n}
whose machines are projected from
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
, we introduce a method to compose
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{1},\ldots,{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{n}
.
This allows for creating large and complex swarms by reusing and composing machines that were previously projected from the individual simpler swarm protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
.
The details behind this broad intuition are quite subtle, due to the highly dynamic nature of swarm protocols, swarms, and their properties – and to achieve these results, we develop novel swarm-specific notions of
well-formedness
and
compositionality
.

In
Section

2
we provide an overview of our approach.

Section

3
presents our new swarm protocol composition and well-formedness, with a method for computing
well-formed subscriptions.
Section

4
defines a projection of swarm protocols onto machines,
a
branch-tracking
semantics for such machines, and proves their eventual fidelity to well-formed swarm protocols.

Section

5
presents our results on correctly composing swarms which realise different
swarm protocols.
In
Section

6
we discuss the companion software artifact of this paper
[
26
]
: an extension of the open source Actyx toolkit that implements the theory of
Sections

3
to
5

to allow the practical compositional development of swarms; we compare experimental results on different strategies for computing the subscriptions of composed swarms.
We discuss related work in
Section

7
and conclude in
Section

8
.

2
Overview and Main Results

Before presenting our development, in

Section

2.1
we give an intuitive account of the swarm framework
in
[
41
]
.
Then, in
Section

2.2
we
outline our approach and summarise our main results.

2.1
Swarms and Swarm Protocols

As mentioned in
Section

1
, a
swarm
is an ensemble of

machines
that can make progress independently without requiring
up-to-date global information or an always-active network connection.
Each machine
\cdot
maintains a
local log

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}}_{\cdot}
of

events
received from the surrounding swarm.
These events are unique and have a globally agreed-upon total
order
[
15
]
(usually defined in a coordination-free manner using timestamps,
which we leave implicit) and are used by
\cdot
to update its state when
newly arriving events alter
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}}_{\cdot}
.

A
swarm protocol
provides a
global view of the intended interactions that should take place during the swarm
execution.
To ensure correct interaction at run-time, each swarm machine should play a
specific
role
\mathtt{R}
in .
To this end, a machine can be obtained by
projecting
onto
\mathtt{R}
; the
projection operation is based on the
subscription
of role
\mathtt{R}
, i.e.,
which event types a machine playing role
\mathtt{R}
observes while running.
E.g., the machine in
Figure

2
is obtained by projecting
the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
protocol in
Figure

1
onto role

\mathtt{D}
using a subscription that associates
\mathtt{D}
with
all the event types of the protocol except
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}
(emitted by

\mathtt{FL}
).
The event emission
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}!
in state
{0}
adds an event of type

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
to the machine’s local log and
leaves
the machine in state
{0}
, whereas accepting
an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
from the log, denoted as

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}?
, transitions the machine to state
{3}
.

For an intuitive presentation of the execution of a swarm realising
the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
protocol, let us consider a possible run of a
swarm of three machines —
\cdot_{\mathtt{T}}
,
\cdot_{\mathtt{FL}}
, and

\cdot_{\mathtt{D}}
— respectively implementing roles
\mathtt{T}
,

\mathtt{FL}
, and
\mathtt{D}
:

1.

Machine
\cdot_{\mathtt{T}}
performs a
request
and emits the event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq_{1}}}
(of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
),
which it adds to its local log
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}}_{\mathtt{T}}
.

2.

This event is asynchronously propagated to the local log
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}}_{\mathtt{FL}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq_{1}}}
of
\cdot_{\mathtt{FL}}
, thus making

\cdot_{\mathtt{FL}}
reach state 1 from where it delivers the part
and emits
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos_{X}}}
(of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}
).

3.

The event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos_{X}}}
reaches
\cdot_{\mathtt{T}}
, which picks up the part
and emits the event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK_{1}}}
(of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
).

4.

These events propagate to machine
\cdot_{\mathtt{D}}
, who adds them to its local log

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}}_{\mathtt{D}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq_{1}}}\logcat\penalty{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos_{X}}}}\logcat\penalty{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK_{1}}}}
.
It then closes the door emitting the event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{8PM}}
(of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
).

Remarkably, when deploying a swarm, multiple machines may assume the same role
in , in a way that generalises
replicated state
machines

[
47
]
.
In our
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
protocol for instance, other machines may play role

\mathtt{FL}
in addition to
\cdot_{\mathtt{FL}}
. Such a machine may concurrently deliver another part by emitting an event, say
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos_{Y}}}
;
eventually, all events propagate to all machines, and all machines
reach the same log.
For instance, the log

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq_{1}}}\logcat\penalty{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos_{X}}}}\logcat\penalty{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos_{Y}}}}\logcat\penalty{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK_{1}}}}\logcat\penalty{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{8PM}}}\logcat\penalty{}

represents a case where event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos_{Y}}}
is emitted after
event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos_{X}}}
; therefore
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos_{Y}}}
will be ignored
by all machines.

Following
[
41
]
, the key correctness
property of a swarm with respect to a protocol is

eventual fidelity
.
Intuitively, eventual fidelity means that once the events emitted by the
machines in propagate to the whole swarm, all machines in
reach a consensus corresponding to a valid execution of .
Notably,
[
41
]
proves that eventual fidelity is guaranteed by construction whenever is

well-formed
and the machines in are correct projections of the roles in ;
in other words, the swarm does not need any runtime orchestration to be eventually faithful to .

2.2
Achieving Compositional Swarms

Unfortunately, as discussed in
Section

1
, the theory and results in
[
41
]
do not feature any
form of compositionality and do not support modular swarm development.
In this work, our key contribution is a new approach enabling the compositional design,
implementation and verification of swarms.
This is a challenging endeavour that we tackle by introducing a brand new
notion of well-formedness that (
i
) ensures eventual fidelity of a swarm to a protocol,
and (
ii
) is preserved by composition (unlike the well-formedness in
[
41
]
).
In
Fig.

5
we provide an overview of our approach
showing how its main constructions are related and how they interact;
the overview leverages the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
composition use cases in
Section

1
.

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
\mathtt{T}
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}^{\prime}
1

Composition

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}

12
