Figure 6
:
The composition
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
.

The pivotal notion upon which we develop our theory is

interfacing roles
, i.e., roles shared by protocols which
“agree” on common event types.
For instance, the transport role
\mathtt{T}
is an interface
between the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
.
Our composition operator
\_\,||\,\_
(
1
in

Fig.

5
) essentially acts as a synchronised product of
the behaviour of the protocols involved in the composition.
For instance, consider the composition

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
shown in
Fig.

6
.
All actions performed by the interfacing role
\mathtt{T}
(i.e.,

{\mathtt{T}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}}\rangle
and
{\mathtt{T}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}}\rangle
) are synchronised
between
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
while actions of non-interfacing
roles (i.e.,
{\mathtt{A}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}}\rangle
and
{\mathtt{D}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}}\rangle
)
are interleaved.
Note that (
i
) the interleaving of
{\mathtt{A}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}}\rangle
and

{\mathtt{D}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}}\rangle
shows that they are concurrent and (
ii
)
the loop in
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
disappears from the composed protocol
since
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
requires the delivery of just one part.

We develop a novel
compositional
notion of well-formedness
for swarm protocols (
2
).
This notion provides a sufficient condition for
statically verifying that a swarm is
eventually faithful to a protocol
(
3
).
As part of this development, we refine the
semantics of machines w.r.t.
[
41
]
by introducing a new
branch-tracking
mechanism. Specifically, in our new machine semantics, each emitted event contains a pointer to the most recent
branching
,
joining
, or
looping

event (
Definition

6
) that precedes
it. This pointer establishes an
event causality chain
and allows machines
to ignore events which are invalid because they are not caused by the expected branching/joining/looping event
(this is illustrated in
Example

29
).
Such a causality chain was not present nor needed in
[
41
]

– but it is necessary for swarms to work correctly with our
compositional well-formedness, which removes restrictions that were assumed in
[
41
]
.
Therefore, the new branch-tracking semantics allows us generalise the correctness results in
[
41
]
;
moreover, it allows us to reduce the size of subscriptions needed to correctly track protocol
choices (
4
), thus increasing the efficiency of the swarm.
Crucially, well-formedness and branch tracking ensure that a swarm of machines projected from a protocol is eventually faithful to (
5
).

Another contribution of this paper is the definition and implementation
of
Algorithm

1
(
6
) to compute
well-formed subscriptions for the composition of a set of input swarm protocols; crucially,
Algorithm

1
computes the subscriptions based on the individual structures of the input protocols
(e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
)
without computing their composition (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
) as it can be exponentially larger than the input protocols
(
7
).

Finally, we develop a composition and adaptation of machines in a swarm, in such a way that the composed swarm (
8
) is eventually faithful to the corresponding composition of swarm protocols.

3
Composing Swarm Protocols

In this section we introduce the composition operation on swarm protocols
(
Section

3.1
)
and our new notion of
well-formedness
(
Section

3.2
).
We then provide an effective method for computing subscriptions that
guarantee the well-formedness of a protocol with respect to those
subscriptions (
Section

3.3

and
Theorem

16
).
First, as in
[
41
]
, we formalise a
swarm protocol

(or simply
protocol
) as a regular term from the
following coinductive grammar:
2
2

2

A term is regular if it consists of finitely many
distinct

subterms. This condition ensures that the language generated by the
co-inductive grammar is finitely representable either using the
so-called “
\mu
notation”
[
46
]
or as solutions of
finite sets of equations
[
19
]
. Also, there is a
correspondence between these structures and finite-state automata;
the interested reader is referred to
[
19
]
.

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{co}}}{{\ ::=\ }}\;\sum_{i\in I}\mathtt{R}_{i}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\rangle\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}\qquad\text{where ${\mathtt{}_{1}}$, ${\mathtt{}_{2}}$, etc. range over roles}

The protocol above specifies that (any machine implementing) role

{\mathtt{}_{i}}
is expected to emit an event of type
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}_{i}
(for some
i\in I
)
and let the protocol continue as
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
.
The summation operator
\sum
represents a nondeterministic choice, so the order of summands is immaterial.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}={{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}
if
I
is empty.
For readability we use the infix notation for summations; for
instance, for the protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
in

Figure

1
we write:

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}={\mathtt{T}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}}\rangle\cdot{\mathtt{FL}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}}\rangle\cdot{\mathtt{T}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}}\rangle\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\;+\;{\mathtt{D}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}}\rangle\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}

3.1
Protocol Interface and Composition

We introduce some convenient
notation: given a protocol

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}=\sum_{i\in I}\mathtt{R}_{i}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\rangle\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
, we write

\mathtt{R}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\rangle\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
if
\mathtt{R}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\rangle
occurs anywhere in (and likewise for

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
, and
\mathtt{R}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
).

In
Definition

1
below we formalise when two protocols
interface
;
intuitively, this happens when event types common to the protocols
are emitted by common roles only.

Definition 1

(Protocol interface)
.

Two protocols and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
are
interfacing
iff for any
\mathtt{R}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\rangle\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
and

{\mathtt{R^{\prime}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}\rangle\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
, it holds that
\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{R}^{\prime}
. The set of
interfacing roles
is

\left\{\mathtt{R}\;\middle|\;\mathtt{R}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\land\mathtt{R}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\right\}
. When
{\mathtt{R}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}\rangle\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
and
\mathtt{R}
is an interfacing role, we say
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
is an
interfacing event type
.

Definition

2
below formalises a protocol composition
operator
\_\,||\,\_
that allows two protocols to run independently (items 1 and 2) – except for the

synchronisations
imposed by the events from their interfacing roles, if any (item 3).

Definition 2

(Protocol composition)
.

Given protocols

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}=\sum_{i\in I}\mathtt{R}_{i}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\rangle\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in J}{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}^{\prime}_{j}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{j}}}}\rangle\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{j}
as well as a
set of roles
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
, we coinductively define:

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\hspace{4em}}r}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle co}}{{:=}}&\sum\{\mathtt{R}_{i}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\rangle\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime})\;\big|\;i\in I,\mathtt{R}_{i}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}\}\hfil\hskip 40.00006pt&(1)\\
&+&\sum\{{\mathtt{R}^{\prime}_{j}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{j}}\rangle\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{j})\;\big|\;j\in J,\mathtt{R}^{\prime}_{j}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}\}\hfil\hskip 40.00006pt&(2)\\
&+&\sum\{\mathtt{R}_{i}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\rangle\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{j})\;\big|\;i\in I,j\in J,\mathtt{R}_{i}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\rangle={\mathtt{R}^{\prime}_{j}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{j}}\rangle\}\hfil\hskip 40.00006pt&(3)\end{array}

We let
\_\,||\,\_
be left-associative and simply write
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}

instead of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}

when
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
is the set of roles that and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
interface on.

By construction,
\_\,||\,\_
is an idempotent and commutative internal operation on
protocols.
Note that we do not introduce new protocol-level syntax for composed
protocols: more precisely, the result of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
is a
protocol obtained by interleaving event types that and

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
do not share while synchronising shared event types.
3
3

3

This design decision allows us us to maintain backward compatibility
with existing tools (see
Section

6
).

Example 3
.

The swarm protocol composition
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
informally depicted
in
Figure

6
is obtained by expanding

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{\{\mathtt{T}\}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}

\displaystyle\;=\;{\mathtt{\mathtt{T}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{\mathtt{\mathtt{FL}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{\mathtt{\mathtt{T}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\big({\mathtt{\mathtt{A}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}+\,{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{\mathtt{\mathtt{A}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\;\;+\;{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}\

∎

In general, when two swarm protocols are composed, only those interfacing event
types emitted in both protocols in the same order are included in the
composition; if interfacing event types are missing in a component protocol or
emitted in different orders, then the composition excludes behaviour present in
the component protocols, as shown in
Example

4
.
4
4

4

Such behaviour restrictions are not consequential for our theoretical
development. If undesired, they can be identified by checking whether the
relevant transitions of and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
remain present in
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
.

Example 4

(Protocol composition and behaviour restrictions)
.

Consider the swarm protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
in
Figure

1
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory^{\prime}}}={\mathtt{\mathtt{T}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{\mathtt{\mathtt{T}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{\mathtt{\mathtt{A}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}
.

By
Definition

2
, the composition
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory^{\prime}}}

yields

{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}

because the interfacing role
\mathtt{T}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory^{\prime}}}
emits
an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
before those of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}

– whereas
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
follows the opposite order.
As another example, letting
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}=\{\mathtt{T}\}
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}={\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}\langle{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}}}}}}\rangle\,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\cdot}\penalty\,{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}

– because, by
Definition

2
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathcal{R}}

“blocks” the actions of role
\mathtt{T}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
as it cannot
synchronise with
{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}
.
∎

3.2
Well-Formedness of Swarm Protocols and Subscriptions

We now introduce our new notion of
well-formedness
(
Definition

12
),
which builds upon the following three properties:

confusion-freeness
(
Definition

5
),

causal consistency
(
Definition

8
), and

determinacy
(
Definition

10
).
Although these three properties have the same name as those
in
[
41
]
, their definitions deviate
significantly to accommodate compositionality. Crucially, our well-formedness is more general than that
of
[
41
]

(except in some minor corner cases discussed later in
Footnote

10
),
hence we do not lose expressiveness.

Before proceeding, we introduce some additional notation. Given
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}=\sum_{i\in I}\mathtt{R}_{i}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\rangle\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
, the predicate

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}_{i}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}}}}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
holds for all
i\in I

and indicates that some role
\mathtt{R}_{i}
can emit an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}
causing the swarm protocol to continue as
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
instead of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}}}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
when the role
\mathtt{R}
is immaterial.
We sometimes write
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}}}\rangle}
when
there is
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}}}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
, and similarly for

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}
. We write
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
if is a
subterm
of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
.
We shorten

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}

to
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
; therefore,
given a sequence of event types
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}
, we write
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}

if
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
.

By
Definition

5
a protocol is
confusion-free
if:
(
1
) every event type is
emitted by only one role;
(
2
) event types are deterministic,
i.e., an event emision leads to only one possible continuation;
and (
3
) the same event type can be
emitted by different subterms only due to concurrency, i.e., any
subterm accounts for a different interleaving of independent event
types.
5
5

5

Item
3
of

Definition

5
can be expensive to check on an
arbitrary , but is immediate when the appropriate

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
are given upfront (which is the common case when
composing protocols).

Definition 5

(Confusion-Freeness)
.

A protocol is
confusion-free
if:

1.

For every
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
, if

{\mathtt{R}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}}}\rangle,{\mathtt{R^{\prime}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}}}\rangle\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
, then

\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{R^{\prime}}
.

2.

For every

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
, if

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}
then
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}
.

3.

There are

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
for
n\geq 1
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
and for every
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}

and
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, there is at most one subterm

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}
.

In
Definition

6
below we characterise four categories of event types in a confusion-free protocol:
(1)
concurrent
event types (i.e., those that are causally independent, see
Figure

7
);
(2)
branching
event types (i.e., those that introduce decision points, see
Figure

8
);
(3)
joining
event types (i.e., those that synchronize multiple concurrent branches, see
Figure

7
);
(4)
looping
event types (i.e., those that are repeated due to loops, see
Figure

9
).
Notice that these categories are not mutually exclusive:
for instance, an event type can be both joining and branching, or neither.

Definition 6

(Concurrent, Branching, Joining, and Looping Event Types)
.

Let be a confusion-free protocol. An event type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
is:

1.

Concurrent with a
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
if there exist

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2}
.
6
6

6

Note that our definition of “concurrent events” together with confusion-freeness (
Definition

5
) ensure that for all
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{a},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{b}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{a},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{b}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{a},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{b}
are concurrent and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{a}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{a}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{b}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{b}
, we also have
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{a}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{b}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{a}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{b}
.

2.

Branching with a
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
at
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}

if there are
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}

with
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
are not
concurrent.

3.

Joining for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}
at
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}
if there are

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2},\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}
, and

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}
are concurrent,
but neither of them is concurrent with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
.

4.

Looping
if there are
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
.

{\mathtt{R^{\prime}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t^{\prime}}}}\rangle
{\mathtt{R^{\prime\prime}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t^{\prime\prime}}}}\rangle
{\mathtt{R^{\prime\prime}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t^{\prime\prime}}}}\rangle
{\mathtt{R^{\prime}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t^{\prime}}}}\rangle
{\mathtt{R}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}\rangle

Figure 7
:
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t^{\prime}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t^{\prime\prime}}}
are concurrent;
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
is joining for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t^{\prime}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t^{\prime\prime}}}
.

{\mathtt{R}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}\rangle
{\mathtt{R^{\prime}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t^{\prime}}}}\rangle

Figure 8
:
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
is branching with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
.

{\mathtt{R}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}\rangle
{\mathtt{R^{\prime}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t^{\prime}}}}\rangle

Figure 9
:
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
are looping.

A subscriptions
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
(
Definition

7
below) specifies which roles observe which event types;

Definition

8
then defines when a swarm protocol is causally consistent w.r.t. a subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
.
Intuitively, causal consistency enforces two key requirements.
First, each role must subscribe to the event types it emits: this will be necessary
later, when we define how to
project
machines from based on
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
.
7
7

7

As mentioned in
Section

2.1
, and later formalised in

Definition

25
, if a machine playing role
\mathtt{R}

emits an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
, that machine will
not
change state unless it
has an accepting transition for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
. Subscribing
\mathtt{R}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
ensures that the projection of a
swarm protocol onto its roles (using
Definition

21
) will
produce machines that can change state by accepting every event type they
emit.
Second, to follow the causal dependencies specified in the protocol, roles must subscribe to the event types directly preceding
the events they emit.

Definition 7

(Subscription)
.

A
subscription

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
is a mapping from roles to sets of event types;
i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
is the set of event types that role
\mathtt{R}
subscribes to.

Definition 8

(Causal-consistent swarm protocol)
.

A protocol
is causal-consistent for a subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}

if, for all
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime},{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}}}\rangle\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
and

2.

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
if
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
are not
concurrent and there is
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}}}\rangle}
.

Example 9

(Causal consistency)
.

Consider the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
protocol in
Figure

4
.
By causal consistency, the subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{A})

of the
\mathtt{A}
ssembly robot must include event types
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
– respectively by the first and second condition of
Definition

8
.
∎

Definition

10
below characterises subscriptions of
determinate

protocols, namely those where each role
\mathtt{R}
subscribes to the events that
are crucial for tracking causality in a swarm protocol .
Intuitively, depending on the category of an event type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
in (branching, joining, or looping),
Definition

10

require that (
i
) role
\mathtt{R}
must subscribe to any event types that are branching with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
(clause
Branching
), (
ii
)
\mathtt{R}
must
subscribe to
any concurrent event types that immediatly precede
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
(clause
Joining
), and (
iii
) each role
\mathtt{R}
must observe each loop iteration,
hence there must be at least one event type in each loop that all roles subscribe to (clause
Looping
).

Definition

10
relies on the set
\mathit{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}},{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma})
, which is the set of roles that
subscribe to event types in that causally depend on
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
according to
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
.
Formally,

\displaystyle\mathit{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}},{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma})=\{\mathtt{R}\;\big|\;

\displaystyle\text{there are }n\geq 0,\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{0},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n},\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}_{n}\text{ such that: }

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{0}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},\;{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{0}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}_{1}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}_{n}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}}\text{ and }

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R}),\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i+1}\text{ not concurrent for all }0\leq i<n\}.

Definition 10

(Determinate protocol subscriptions)
.

A protocol
is determinate for subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
if,
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},\mathtt{R}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}

Branching:

If
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
is branching with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
at
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
and

\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime},)
, then

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
.

Joining:

If
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
is joining for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}
at
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
and

\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime},)
, then

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
.

Looping:

If
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
for some non-empty
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}

then there are
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
for all

\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime\prime},)
. We call
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
looping in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
.

Example 11

(Branching vs. looping events)
.

In
Figure

1
, the loop

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}

can be exited by event type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
, which branches with

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
. Consequently, subscribing to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
is required by clause
branching
of

Definition

10
– and the subscription to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
also
satisfies the clause
looping
.
In general, if a protocol contains a loop with a branching that exits the
loop, then each branching event type that continues the loop is also a
looping event type in the subscription (by
Definition

6
) and
subscribing to it satisfies clause
looping
of
Definition

10
.
∎

We now have all the ingredients to formalise when a swarm protocol is well-formed.

Definition 12

(Well-formed swarm protocols)
.

A protocol is
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed
if it is
confusion-free, causal-consistent in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
, and determinate for

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}

(
Definitions

5
,
10
and
8
).

Example 13

(Well-formed protocol and subscription)
.

The protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
in
Example

3

and
Figure

6
is confusion-free (by

Definition

5
). Now, take the assembly robot
\mathtt{A}
and a subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed
(by
Definition

12
): rule (1) of causal consistency
(
Definition

8
) requires

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{A})
, implying

\mathtt{A}\in\mathit{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}},)
;
therefore, from the
branching
rule of determinacy
(
Definition

10
), we have

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{A})
. The

joining
and
looping
rules of
Definition

10
hold
vacuously, as there are no joining event types or loops in

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
.∎

3.3
On Computing Subscriptions

This section introduces
Algorithm

1
for computing subscriptions that guarantee
well-formedness (by
Definition

12
) for a composition
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
of given swarm protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
. In general, for any confusion-free swarm protocol there is at least one
subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
such that is
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed:
it is the “total” subscription where each role in subscribes to all event types in .
However, if such a ‘‘total’’ subscription is used to project machines from
(using
Definition

21
later on), then the resulting swarm would consist of complex machines that await
the emission of all events emitted by all other machines, leading to inefficient executions.
It is hence worthwhile to compute smaller subscriptions that preserve
well-formedness.
Importantly, for a composition
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
of
n

composable
protocols (by
Definition

14
below),
our
Algorithm

1
operates on the individual input protocols without expanding their composition (
Definition

2
),
because the size of the expanded protocol may grow exponentially with
n
(as shown later in
Section

6.2
).

Definition 14

(Composable swarm protocols)
.

A protocol is
sequential
if it
does not contain concurrent events. A set of protocols is
composable
if they
are pairwise interfacing and each protocol is sequential and confusion-free.

Algorithm

1
takes a set
\{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
composable protocols together with a set
\{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
subscriptions, and generates a subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed and

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{i}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
holds for all
i\in I
.

Input:

A set
\{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}\}
of composable protocols and a set of subscriptions
\{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{n}\}

Output:

A subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}\supseteq{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{n}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed.

Let
\mathit{subscribers}(,)
denote the roles that subscribe to some event type of in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
.

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}:=\bigcup_{i\leq n}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{i}

\mathtt{ifr}:=\{\mathtt{R}\mid\exists i,j\leq n:i\neq j\land\mathtt{R}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}\land\mathtt{R}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{j}\}

\mathtt{conc}:=\{\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{j}\}\mid i,j\leq n,\,\mathtt{R}_{i}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\rangle\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i},\,\mathtt{R}_{j}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{j}\rangle\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{j},\,\mathtt{R}_{i}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{j},\,\mathtt{R}_{j}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}\}

repeat

for all

i\leq n
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i},\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
, and
\mathtt{R},\,\mathtt{R}^{\prime},\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},\,{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}

do

Causal Consistency:
If
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{\mathtt{R}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\rangle}
, then add
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
; If
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\mathtt{R}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\rangle}
, then add
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
.

Branching:
If
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
is branching with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
at
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{subscribers}({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i},)
, then add
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
.

Joining:
For all
j\leq n
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{j}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{j}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{j}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{j}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}
,
\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}\}\in\mathtt{conc}
,
\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\}\not\in\mathtt{conc}
,
\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\}\not\in\mathtt{conc}
, and
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{subscribers}({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i},)
: Add
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime},\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime},\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
.

Interfacing:
If
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{\mathtt{R}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\rangle}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
with
\mathtt{R}\in\mathtt{ifr}
and
\mathtt{R}^{\prime}\in\mathit{subscribers}({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime\prime}_{i},)
, then add
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R}^{\prime})
.

end

for

until

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
no longer changes

for all

i\leq n
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
, such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}
, and
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{subscribers}({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i},)

do

Looping:
If
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}
has no looping event type in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
, then add
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
.

end

for

Algorithm 1

Computing a subscription for a composition of protocols.

The algorithm initialises three sets:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
, initially approximating the result as the union of the
given
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{i}
. Each
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{i}
can be understood as the
specification of the event types that each role in
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
is interested in.

•

\mathtt{ifr}
containing all interfacing roles.

•

\mathtt{conc}
containing all potentially concurrent event types, i.e.,
the pairs of event types belonging to different components that are emited
by non-interfacing roles.

The algorithm iteratively enlarges
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
by applying monotone
operations corresponding to the definitions of causal-consistency
(
Definition

8
) and
branching
and
joining
of
determinacy (
Definition

10
), together with the new operator

interfacing
, which is crucial for ensuring the correct synchronisation
of the protocols (as will be discussed below).
The iteration terminates when a fixed point is reached.
The final phase ensures that every role that is involved in a term that
contains a loop
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}
is subscribed to (at least) one looping event of

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}
.

On the need of the
interfacing
step in
Algorithm

1
.

One of the requirements of well-formedness (
Definition

12
) is
determinacy
(
Definition

10
).
Checking the determinacy of a composed swarm protocol for a subscription requires the set
\mathit{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime},)
for subterms
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}^{\prime}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}^{\prime}
– and computing that set would require the
explicit computation of the expanded protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
(using
Definition

2
);
however, the size of the expanded
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
can be exponential in
n
.
To avoid this exponential blow-up,
Algorithm

1
uses the set
\mathit{subscribers}({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i},)
(where
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}
is a subterm of
one of the input protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
)
and its
interfacing
step to over-approximate the set

\mathit{roles}(,,)
without explicitly expanding
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
.
To see why this approach yields the desired over-approximation, recall that
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime},)
holds if there exists a path
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{0}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}_{1}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}_{n}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{0}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
, and every pair
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i+1}
is not concurrent for
0\leq i<n
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i+1}
are not concurrent, they must either belong to the same concurrency-free input swarm protocol or they are separated by interfacing event types that enforce their ordering.
Consequently, there exists a path
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{0}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}^{\prime}_{1}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}}
satisfying the conditions above, where
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}-1}
are interfacing event types and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{i}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{i+1}
occur within the same input protocol for
0\leq i<n^{\prime}
.
The
interfacing
step propagates subscriptions along this path, that is, if
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{i+1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
, then also
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
.
Starting from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
, repeated application of the
interfacing
step backwards along the path ensures that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{1}
is in the same input protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}
as
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
, we obtain
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{subscribers}({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i},)
.

(This argument is formally stated in
Lemma

39
in
Appendix

A
)
.

Example 15

(Application of
Algorithm

1
to

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
)
.

We illustrate the application of
Algorithm

1
to the
protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
(depicted in

Figures

1
and
4
, respectively) and two empty subscriptions

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{1}={\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{2}=\emptyset
.
It is straightforward to check that these two protocols are composable, since
they are sequential, confusion-free and they interface on
{\mathtt{T}}
.
Then, the algorithm initialises

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}={\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{1}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{2}=\emptyset
,

\mathtt{ifr}=\{{\mathtt{T}}\}
, and

\mathtt{conc}=\{\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}\},\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}\}\}
.
Note that
\mathtt{conc}
over-approximates the actual pairs of concurrent
event types. Indeed, only
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}
are
concurrent in the composition
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
(see

Figure

6
). Now the algorithm starts the iteration with the
step
causal consistency
.
Starting with role
{\mathtt{T}}
and protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
, we first add the event types
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}

to
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}({\mathtt{T}})
because
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
has transitions
where
{\mathtt{T}}
emits such event types.
In
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
, the same role
{\mathtt{T}}
emits

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
after
{\mathtt{FL}}
emits
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}
; consequently

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}
is added to
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}({\mathtt{T}})
. Subscriptions for
the remaining roles, shown below, are obtained analogously.

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}\;=\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathtt{T}\mapsto\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}\},&\mathtt{FL}\mapsto\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}\},\\
\mathtt{D}\mapsto\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}\},&\mathtt{A}\mapsto\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}\}\end{array}\right\}

Then, the
branching
step is executed. Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
are branching in
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
. Therefore, (
i
)

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
is added to the subscriptions of the roles already
subscribed to event types of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
, i.e., to
\mathtt{D}
and

\mathtt{A}
; (
ii
) analogously,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
is added to

\mathtt{T}
,
\mathtt{FL}
, and
\mathtt{A}
.
Step
joining
is not actually applied in this example because its conditions are not satisfied.

Next, the
interfacing
step adds
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
to all roles that have subscribed to some event that follows
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
in some of the protocols; hence,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
is added to
\mathtt{FL}
because it already subscribed to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
.

The fixed point of the iteration is:

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{T},\ \ \mathtt{FL}\ \mapsto\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}\}\\
\mathtt{D}\mapsto\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}\},\quad\mathtt{A}\mapsto\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{car}}\}\\
\end{array}\right\}

In
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
, the event type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
is looping in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
. However, it was already added to the relevant roles due to the branching rule.
Thus, the
looping
phase is not applied.∎

Theorem

16
below states that

Algorithm

1
computes a subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}

ensuring well-formedness of the composed protocol.
(Proof in

Appendix

A
.)

Theorem 16

(Correctness of
Algorithm

1
)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
is the subscription computed from the composable protocols

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
and subscriptions
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{n}

using
Algorithm

1
,

then
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
is

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed.

4
Realising Swarm Protocols as Swarms of Machines

We now address the problem of correctly
realising
a swarm protocol (
Definition

28
), i.e.,
constructing a swarm of machines that interact as specified by . We begin by fixing the language for writing machines (
Section

4.1
).
Following the standard top-down approach for behavioral types, we rely on a
projection
operation (
Section

4.2
) to derive correct machines for the different roles of a protocol. Then, we refine the swarm model introduced
in
[
41
]
by adding a

branch-tracking
mechanism to the semantics of machines (
Section

4.3
) in a swarm (
Section

4.4
).
This revised semantics is essential for (1) ensuring that the machines projected from a swarm protocol are
eventually faithful
to
(
Section

4.5
), and (2)
enabling the composition of swarms (discussed in
Section

5
).

4.1
Machines

A
machine
models a swarm agent that processes a local log of
events to make decisions on the emission of new events.
We define a machine as a grammar term (
Definition

17
) with a corresponding finite-state automaton (
Definition

18
).

Definition 17

(Machine)
.

A
machine
is a
regular term
from the
coinductive grammar:

\cdot\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{co}}}{{\ ::=\ }}\kappa\cdot\&_{i\in I}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,{}_{i}\cdot{}_{i}\qquad\text{where $\kappa$ is a finite set of event types dubbed the \emph{emitter set}}\qquad

such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{j}
for all
i\neq j\in I
.
We write
{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}
when
\kappa=I=\emptyset
.

Definition 18

(Machine states and transitions)
.

A machine
\cdot
from
Definition

17
can be described as the deterministic finite-state automaton where:

•

Each distinct subterm of
\cdot
(finitely many since
\cdot
is regular) corresponds to a
state
.

•

The
initial state
is
\cdot
itself.

•

There is an
event acceptance transition

{}_{1}\cdot{}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,}{}_{2}\cdot{}_{2}

whenever machine
{}_{1}\cdot{}_{1}
has a branch
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,
leading to

{}_{2}\cdot{}_{2}

•

and an
event emission transition

\cdot^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{!}}\cdot^{\prime}
for any
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
in the
emitter set of
\cdot^{\prime}
.

Remark 19
.

The grammar-based presentation of machines (
Definition

17
) and the automaton presentation (
Definition

18
) are equivalent: each distinct subterm corresponds to a state, branches correspond to acceptance transitions, and emitter sets yield emission self-loops.

Example 20
.

By
Definition

18
,
the automaton depicted in
Fig.

2
corresponds to the
machine:
8
8

8

For readability, in our examples we often use brackets and infix
notation for the branching operator
\&
.

\cdot_{\mathtt{D}}\;=\;\{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{closingTime}}}\}\cdot\Bigl((\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{partReq}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{partOK}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,\cdot)\ \&\ (\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{closingTime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}})\Bigr)

Note that a closing-time event can be emitted only
from
\cdot_{\mathtt{D}}
(the initial state in the automaton of
Fig.

2
)
since
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{closingTime}}}
belongs only to the emitter set of
\cdot_{\mathtt{D}}
.
Also note that in
Fig.

2
, emitted events appear as
self-loops
:
this is because (by
Definition

18
above)
a machine remains in its current state while emitting an event;
a state transition may occur only when a machine accepts an event from its log.
∎

4.2
Projecting a Swarm Protocol onto its Roles

By
Definition

21
below, the
projection
of a swarm
protocol onto a role
\mathtt{R}
yields a machine that captures the behaviour
of
\mathtt{R}
in . Specifically, the projected machine
retains the event types that are either emitted or subscribed to
by
\mathtt{R}
– and it disregards all other event types.

Definition 21

(Projection)
.

We coinductively define the
projection
of a
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed swarm protocol onto a role
\mathtt{R}
as:

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}\;\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{co}}}{{:=}}\;\;\kappa\cdot\&\Bigl\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}?({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}})\;\;\Big|\;\;\exists{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}:{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})=\emptyset\;\text{ and }\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})\Bigr\}

where

\kappa=\bigl\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\;\big|\;\exists{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}:{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}}\xrightarrow{\mathtt{R}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}\rangle}\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})=\emptyset\bigr\}
.

Observe that, by
Definition

21
, the initial state of the projected machine
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}
has:

•

A branch
\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,
for every event type that
subscribes to (by
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
) and can be reached from after
a (possibly empty) sequence of events of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}

not
subscribed to by .

•

An emitter set
\kappa
consisting of all the event types that
can emit in after a (possibly empty)
sequence of events of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}

not
subscribed to by
\mathtt{R}
.

Hence, the projection
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}
may start by either
emitting an event of a type in
\kappa
or proceed to

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}
upon accepting an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
.
Note that an
event type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
that is
not
subscribed to by
\mathtt{R}
does
not

appear in
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}
, i.e., the projected machine ignores all events of such a type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
.

Example 22

(Projection)
.

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}

Figure

6
and the following subscription (where
E
stands for
the set of all event types in ):

Figure

.

:

(from

1
) on roles
\mathtt{T},\mathtt{D}
, and
\mathtt{FL}

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
