Example

29
.

Notice that in
Definition

21
,
when constructing the projection
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}

there might be multiple
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
,
meaning the projected machine can accept or emit
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}
and then reach one of potentially many different options for
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}
. Still,
Lemma

23
ensures that if is well-formed, then
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}
relates to only one unique machine.

Lemma 23
.

If is a
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed protocol, then
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}
is uniquely defined for all
.

Proof.

(Outline; full proof in
Appendix

B
)

The
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formedness property ensures that the
projection satisfies
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}

for all sub-terms
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime\prime}
reachable from
by event types not in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
– so it does
not matter which one is chosen in the construction of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}
. This is
because the subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
includes all event types that can
influence the future behaviour of the projection – so in

Definition

21
, the event types selected in
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}
(which
are not in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
)
do not influence the shape of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{R}}
.
∎

4.3
Branch-Tracking Machine Semantics

In this section we define how machines emit events and process their local log of events.
We fix a set of
events
, ranged over by ; each event has a
unique
event type

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
. An
event log

is a sequence
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}}_{1}}\logcat\penalty{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}}_{2}}\logcat\penalty{\ldots}
of
pairwise distinct events.
As anticipated in
Section

2.2
, we incorporate a new
branch-tracking mechanism
to the machine semantics
originally proposed in
[
41
]
to establish an explicit causal link between events.
Such a link was not present nor needed in
[
41
]

– but it is necessary for swarms to work correctly with our
compositional well-formedness (
Definition

12
), which removes restrictions assumed by the machine semantics in
[
41
]
.

The behaviour of a machine
\cdot
is determined by a
log-processing
function

\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}
(formalised in
Definition

24
below).
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\mathcal{T}}}
be the set of all event types. In defining
\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}
, we assume that machines are equipped with an additional element, which does not appear in
[
41
]
:
A set of
updating event types

{\cdot}.\!\operatorname{UP}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\mathcal{T}}}
.
9
9

9

We will see in
Definition

28
that this set is populated with branching, joining, and looping event types from a swarm protocol.

If an event has a type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in{\cdot}.\!\operatorname{UP}
, we call an
updating event
.
We also add to each event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
a field
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}.\mathsf{last_{up}}
, which intuitively refers to the last updating event that caused
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
.
A machine
\cdot
processes its log starting from the oldest event. When processing an event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
,
\cdot
performs a transition if:

1.

There is an acceptance transition
\cdot\xrightarrow{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,}\cdot^{\prime}
; i.e.,
\cdot
accepts events of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
; and

2.

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}.\mathsf{last_{up}}
coincides with the last updating event observed by
\cdot
; i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}.\mathsf{last_{up}}
is the last updating event that causally preceded
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
.

If the two conditions above hold, then
\cdot
accepts

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
and moves to the next state
\cdot^{\prime}
, i.e., the continuation of the event acceptance transition, from which the remaning events in the log are processed. Otherwise,
\cdot
skips
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
and processes the next event in the log.

More precisely, in order to remember previously-seen updating events and check the pointer
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}.\mathsf{last_{up}}
, each machine keeps
a partial mapping
\mathsf{last_{up}}:{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\mathcal{T}}}\rightharpoonup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\mathcal{E}}}
that
associates each event type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
with the
last updating event
for the type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
.
The mapping
\mathsf{last_{up}}
is built by the machine while processing a log: initially,
\mathsf{last_{up}}

is empty (i.e.,
\emptyset
) and it is updated every
time a machine processes an updating event.
The updating of
\mathsf{last_{up}}
is performed as follows. Let
\mathsf{branch}({\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}})
be a partial mapping that, for each machine
\cdot
and event type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
, gives the set event types that continue along the same branch as
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
until the next updating event;
specifically, if
\cdot\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\,?}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}\,?}\ldots\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}\,?}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n-1}\notin{\cdot}.\!\operatorname{UP}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}
not concurrent with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}\in\mathsf{branch}({\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}})
.
The processing of an event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
by a machine
\cdot
that has an event acceptance transition

\cdot\xrightarrow{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,}\cdot^{\prime}
is described by the following relation:

\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\quad}l}({},)&\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}}&({\cdot^{\prime}},)&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}.\mathsf{last_{up}}=\mathsf{last_{up}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}})$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\notin{\cdot}.\!\operatorname{UP}$}\\
({},)&\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}}&({\cdot^{\prime}},\mathsf{last_{up}}[\mathsf{branch}({\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}})\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}])&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}.\mathsf{last_{up}}=\mathsf{last_{up}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}})$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in{\cdot}.\!\operatorname{UP}$.}\end{array}

where
\mathsf{last_{up}}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}]
denotes the update of
\mathsf{last_{up}}
, namely the
mapping that maps each event type in the set
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}
to event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
, and behaves as
\mathsf{last_{up}}
for other event types.

The mechanism through which a machine
\cdot
processes a complete event log ,
or equivalently determines its current state based on , is specified by
Definition

24
below.

Definition 24

(Log-processing function)
.

The
log-processing function

\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}

is defined as
\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})={\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}_{BT}}(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}},\emptyset)
where:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}_{BT}}(\cdot,\epsilon,\mathsf{last_{up}})&=&(\cdot,\mathsf{last_{up}})\\
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}_{BT}}(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}},\mathsf{last_{up}})&=&\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}_{BT}}(\cdot^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}},\mathsf{last_{up}}^{\prime})&\text{if $({},)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}}({\cdot^{\prime}},\mathsf{last_{up}}^{\prime})$}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}_{BT}}(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}},\mathsf{last_{up}})&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}\end{array}

Note that in
Definition

24
,
\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}
is defined in terms of the auxiliary
function
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}_{BT}}
, which is in charge of constructing
\mathsf{last_{up}}

while traversing the log.
Observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}_{BT}}
, and consequently
\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}
,
returns a pair
(\cdot^{\prime},\mathsf{last_{up}})
where
\cdot^{\prime}
is the reached machine and

\mathsf{last_{up}}
is the mapping built after processing the log.
Based on the current state computed by the log-processing function
\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}
, machines generate new events according to
Definition

25
below.

Definition 25

(Operational semantics of machines)
.

A machine
\cdot
with a local log emits events according to the following rule:

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\textstyle\rule[-5.59721pt]{0.0pt}{12.91663pt}\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})=(\kappa\cdot\&_{i\in I}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,{}_{i}\cdot{}_{i},\ \mathsf{last_{up}})\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in\kappa\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}\text{ has type }{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}.\mathsf{last_{up}}=\mathsf{last_{up}}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}})}{\textstyle\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{12.91663pt}(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}!}(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}})}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{\footnotesize{\sc[emit]}}\end{array}

The transition

(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}!}(\cdot,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}})
in
Definition

25
reads as:

“a machine
\cdot
with a log emits an event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
.”

Observe that the new event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
is added to the end of the local log, and the transition is possible only if, after processing , the machine reaches a state
\kappa\cdot\&_{i\in I}\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}?}\,{}_{i}\cdot{}_{i}
where the emission of events of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
is enabled (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in\kappa
). Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
must be of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
and must reference the last updating event for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
.

4.4
Swarms

A
swarm
(of size
n
) is a pair
({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})
where:

•

maps indices
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
to machines and their local
log, i.e.
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(i)=({}_{i}\cdot{}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i})
;

•

is a
global log
, i.e., a sequence consisting of all
events generated by the machines in that preserves their order of
generation. More formally:

–

Every log (global or local)

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}}_{n}
totally orders its events using a relation
<_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}^{\prime}}
defined as

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}}_{i}<_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}}_{j}
iff
i<j
;

–

The global log contains all and only the events of the local logs
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i}
(for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
) and preserves their respective ordering:
\bigcup_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}
and
<_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i}}\subseteq<_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}}
.

For brevity, we may write instead of
({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})
(and call a swarm) if the global log is empty.

Remark 26
.

The global log of a swarm
({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})
does not exist in
actual swarm implementations: it is a formalisation device which models the global ordering and
non-deterministic propagation of events, as defined by swarm semantics formalised in

Definition

27
below.

Definition 27

(Swarm semantics)
.

The behaviour of a swarm is defined by the smallest relation closed under
the following rules:

\frac{\textstyle\rule[-5.59721pt]{0.0pt}{12.91663pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(i)=({}_{i}\cdot{}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i})\quad({}_{i}\cdot{}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i})\xRightarrow{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}!}({}_{i}\cdot{}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}})}{\textstyle\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{12.91663pt}({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}^{\prime})\xlongrightarrow{{\mathtt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{{}}}}!}({{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}}[{i}\mapsto{({}_{i}\cdot{}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}})}],{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}^{\prime})}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{\footnotesize{\sc[Local]}}\qquad\frac{\textstyle\rule[-5.59721pt]{0.0pt}{12.91663pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(i)=({}_{i}\cdot{}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i})\quad<_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i}}\subset<_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}^{\prime}}\subseteq<_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}}}{\textstyle\rule[-2.15277pt]{0.0pt}{12.91663pt}({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})\xlongrightarrow{\tau}({{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}}[{i}\mapsto{({}_{i}\cdot{}_{i},{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}^{\prime}})}],{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{\footnotesize{\sc[Prop]}}

In
Definition

27
, rule
Local

formalises the emission of an event enabled at the
machine at
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(i)
of the swarm
({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})
. The emitted
event is appended to the local log of
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(i)
and
non-deterministically merged in the global log respecting the order of
events previously generated by
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(i)
.
Rule
Prop
models the asynchronous
event log propagation among machines: for some machine
{}_{i}\cdot{}_{i}
with a local log

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i}
(that is strictly included in the global log ), the
rule non-deterministically selects a log

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}
that contains
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i}
and transfers the events in
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}^{\prime}
to

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}_{i}
while preserving their order.
Note that during an execution, machines can emit events
independently, and their local log may only partially reflect the “global state”
in the global log (due to the non-deterministic and asynchronous
propagation of events).

Definition

28
below formalises the
realisation
of a swarm protocol , i.e.,
a swarm whose machines interact according to the roles .

Definition 28

(Realisation of a swarm protocol)
.

A swarm
({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}},\epsilon)
of size
n

realises
a protocol
with respect to a subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
iff for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, (
i
)
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(i)=({}_{i}\cdot{}_{i},\epsilon)
, and
(ii)

{}_{i}\cdot{}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}
for some
\mathtt{R}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
; and (
iii
)
{{}_{i}\cdot{}_{i}}.\!\operatorname{UP}
is
equal to the event types of that are branching, joining, or looping in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
.
We also say that
{}_{i}\cdot{}_{i}
realises
\mathtt{R}
.

Example 29

(Swarm protocol realisation and execution)
.

Let
E
denote the set of all event types in the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
protocol (
Figure

1
) and
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}=\{\mathtt{T}\mapsto E,\mathtt{FL}\mapsto E\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}\},\mathtt{D}\mapsto E\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}\}\}
.
By
Definition

12
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed.
By
Definition

28
, the swarm
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}
below realises
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
:

{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}\;=\;\left\{i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{1}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},\epsilon),\quad i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{2}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},\epsilon),\quad i_{{\mathtt{FL}}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}},\epsilon),\quad i_{{\mathtt{D}}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{D}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{D}},\epsilon)\right\}

where
{}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{T}}}
,
{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{FL}}}
,
and
{}_{\mathtt{D}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{D}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}
(shown in
Figure

11
). Notice that the machine in

{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}(i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{1}})
and

{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}(i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{2}})
play the same role

\mathtt{T}
.
By
Definition

27
, a possible execution of the swarm
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}
is:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}r@{\;}l@{\quad}c@{\;}r@{\;}c@{\;}l}&({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}},\epsilon)\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}!}&({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1})&&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{1}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}{[i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{1}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1})]}\\
&\xRightarrow{\tau}&({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{2},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1})&&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{2}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{1}{[i_{{\mathtt{FL}}_{1}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1})]}\\
&\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}!}&({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{3},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2})&&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{3}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{2}{[i_{\mathtt{FL}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2})]}\\
&\xRightarrow{\tau}&({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{4},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2})&&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{4}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{3}{[i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{1}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2})]}\\
&\xRightarrow{\tau}&({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{5},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2})&&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{5}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{4}{[i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{2}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2})]}\\
&\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}!}&({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{6},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{3})&&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{6}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{5}{[i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{1}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{3})]}\\
&\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}!}&({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{7},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{3}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4})&&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{7}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{6}{[i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{1}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{3}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4})]}\\
&\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}!}&({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{8},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})&&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{8}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{7}{[i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{2}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{5})]}\\
&\xRightarrow{\tau}^{*}&({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{9},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})&&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{9}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{8}{[i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{1}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}),i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{2}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}),i_{{\mathtt{FL}}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}),i_{{\mathtt{D}}}\mapsto({}_{\mathtt{D}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{D}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})]}\\
\end{array}

where, in states
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{8}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{9}
, the global log is

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{3}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{5}

and its events carry the following pointers:

\begin{array}[]{rclllllll}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}.\mathsf{last_{up}}&=&\perp\text{(undefined)}&&&&&&\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2}.\mathsf{last_{up}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{3}.\mathsf{last_{up}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4}.\mathsf{last_{up}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{5}.\mathsf{last_{up}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\end{array}

In each state of the swarm, we compute the state of a machine for its local log
using
\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}

(
Definition

24
), which in turn uses the
branching, joining, and looping events in to update the mapping

\mathsf{last_{up}}
and only considers events carrying correct pointers.
For this example, when the machines process the event

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4}
, they will update their mapping
\mathsf{last_{up}}
to
obtain:

\mathsf{last_{up}}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOk}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4}]

In this updated mapping, all event types of

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
point to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4}
, which means
that the machines will
only accept new events
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}.\mathsf{last_{up}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4}
.
Observe that, in the execution above, the event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{5}
was emitted by the machine with ID
i_{{\mathtt{T}}_{2}}

in response to the event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}
.
Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{5}.\mathsf{last_{up}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4}
;
i.e., the event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{5}
is causally linked to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}

through branch tracking.
Since the machines expect events,
whose
\mathsf{last_{up}}
fields refer to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4}
,
the event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{5}
is ignored by all
machines after the logs propagate.
Once this happens in state
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{9}
,
the machines reach the states:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}({}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.1843,0.2431,0.9176}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}}})&=&({\mathtt{FL}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}}\rangle.{\mathtt{T}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}}\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}})\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{T}}}\\
\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}({}_{\mathtt{D}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{D}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}})&=&({\mathtt{T}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}}\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}})\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}\\
\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}({}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}},{\color[rgb]{0.1843,0.2431,0.9176}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}}})&=&({\mathtt{FL}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}}\rangle.{\mathtt{T}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}}\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}})\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{FL}}}\end{array}

Here, the machines
{}_{\mathtt{T}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{T}}
and
{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}}

expect the forklift to
emit an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}
, while
{}_{\mathtt{D}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{D}}

(which does not subscribe to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}
in

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
) is waiting for an event of type

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
that has the correct pointer and thus follows
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}
in the swarm protocol

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
.
These states are consistent with each other:
({\mathtt{T}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}}\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}})\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}=(({\mathtt{FL}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}}\rangle.{\mathtt{T}}\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}}\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}})\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}
.Thus they correspond
to a valid run of the
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
swarm protocol.
∎

Remark 30
.

Running the swarm of
Example

29
with the semantics
of
[
41
]
can lead to a state where
the machines irremediably diverge due to the absence of branch tracking
and the resulting lack of event causality tracking.
We illustrate this situation in
Appendix

H
.

4.5
Eventual Fidelity of Swarm Protocol Realisations

In this section, we address the problem of ensuring that a swarm behaves correctly with respect to a swarm protocol .
As our correctness criterion, we adopt the notion of

eventual fidelity
(
Definition

31
below).
Intuitively, a swarm is eventually faithful to a protocol if, in every execution, once all machines have received all messages,
they agree on the same
consistent view
of the execution path of .
By consistent view, we mean that machines are able to determine which events belong to the effective execution path through and disregard all other events that may have been emitted inconsistently.
We call such a view the
effective log
(see
Appendix

C
for formal definitions).
When considering a global log generated by a swarm, the effective log w.r.t. , written
\mathtt{eff}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}})
, is the
sublog
of that
consists solely of those events of that follow a valid path through .
We also define the effective log with
respect to a specific machine
\cdot
, written
\mathtt{eff}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}},\cdot)
, as
the sublog of containing only the events that
\cdot
accepts
(i.e., all the events not ignored by the log-processing function
\hat{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}{\delta}}
).

To formalise the eventual fidelity of a swarm to a protocol ,
we assign to every machine in the swarm some role of : we denote this by a mapping
\operatorname{r}
from machines to the roles they play. Eventual fidelity requires that if a machine
\cdot
that plays role
\operatorname{r}(\cdot)
is provided with a global log
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}}
produced by the swarm, then
\cdot
accepts only those events which are in the global effective log and to which
\operatorname{r}(\cdot)
subscribes.
We denote such events by
\mathtt{eff}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}})\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\operatorname{r}(\cdot))}
, which is the projection of
\mathtt{eff}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}})
onto the event types that role
\operatorname{r}(\cdot)
subscribes to in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
.

Definition 31

(Eventual fidelity)
.

A swarm
({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}},\epsilon)
of size
n
is

eventually faithful
to a protocol with respect to a subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
if
for all such that
({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}},\epsilon)\rightarrow^{*}({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}})
, for all
\mathtt{R}\in{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}
, and for all
i\in\{1...n\}
, we have

\mathtt{eff}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}})\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\operatorname{r}({{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(i)))}=\mathtt{eff}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{l}},{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(i))
.

Theorem

32
below ensures that a realisation of a swarm protocol (i.e., a swarm
consisting of machines projected from , by
Definition

28
) is eventually faithful to . The proof is in
Appendix

D
.

Theorem 32

(Eventual fidelity of swarm protocol realisations)
.

If a swarm realises a
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed protocol ,
then is eventually faithful to with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
.

Example 33

(Effective logs and eventual fidelity)
.

Consider the swarm protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
(
Figure

1
),
its projected machine
{}_{\mathtt{D}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{D}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}
,
(
Figure

11
),
and the log from
Example

29
. By
Definitions

41
and
42
, the effective logs for
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}

and
\cdot_{\mathtt{D}}
filtered from are:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathtt{eff}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{pos}}_{2}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{3}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4}\\
\mathtt{eff}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}},\cdot_{\mathtt{D}})&=&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{1}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partOK}}_{3}\logcat{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{partReq}}_{4}\end{array}

Therefore, we have
\mathtt{eff}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}})\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{D})}=\mathtt{eff}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}},\cdot_{\mathtt{D}})
:
hence, the effective logs for the machine and the swarm protocol align, as required by eventual fidelity (
Definition

31
).
∎

5
Composing Machines and Swarms

In this section, we present an approach for realising a composition of swarm protocols

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
by composing swarms of pre-existing machines which realise the individual protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
(for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
). This is crucial for enabling the reuse of pre-existing machines and their deployment in new, larger swarms (that we implement in
Section

6
). We first formalise the composition of machines (
Section

5.1
); then, we introduce an automatic
adaptation
mechanism (
Section

5.2
) for such pre-existing machines, enabling us to compose entire swarms (
Algorithm

2
) while guaranteeing eventual fidelity to the composed swarm protocol (
Theorem

38
).

5.1
Machine Composition

Similar to swarm protocol composition (
Definition

2
), in

Definition

34
below we define the composition of two machines
\cdot
and
\cdot^{\prime}
as a synchronised product. Intuitively, if a transition is common to
\cdot
and
\cdot^{\prime}
, the machines synchronise and the continuation of
\cdot\mathbin{{\,||\,}}\cdot^{\prime}
is given by the composition of both corresponding continuations of
\cdot
and
\cdot^{\prime}
(clause
Common Events
);
otherwise, the continuation of
\cdot\mathbin{{\,||\,}}\cdot^{\prime}
is the composition of
the continuation of either
\cdot
or
\cdot^{\prime}
with the other machine unchanged
(clauses
Events Unique
).

Definition 34

(Machine composition)
.

Let
\cdot:={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\kappa}}\cdot\&_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}?\cdot_{i}
and
\cdot^{\prime}:={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\kappa}}^{\prime}\cdot\&_{j\in J}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{j}^{\prime}?\cdot_{j}^{\prime}
be
machines. Let
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}
be the set of event types that occur in both
\cdot
and
\cdot^{\prime}
.
The
composition of
\cdot
and
\cdot^{\prime}
, written
\cdot\mathbin{{\,||\,}}\cdot^{\prime}
, is defined as
\cdot\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}}}\cdot^{\prime}
, which in turn is coinductively defined:

\cdot\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}}}\cdot^{\prime}\;\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle co}}{{:=}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\kappa}}^{\prime\prime}\cdot\&_{k\in K}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{k}?\cdot_{k}

where
K
is the smallest set such that for all
i\in I,j\in J
:

Common Events (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T},\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{j}^{\prime}
):

There is a
k\in K
with

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{k}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}
and
\cdot_{k}=\cdot_{i}\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}}}\cdot_{j}^{\prime}
.

Events Unique to
\cdot
(
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}
):

There is a
k\in K
with
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{k}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}
and

\cdot_{k}=\cdot_{i}\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}}}\cdot^{\prime}
.

Events Unique to
\cdot^{\prime}
(
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{j}^{\prime}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}
):

There is a
k\in K
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{k}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{j}^{\prime}
and

\cdot_{k}=\cdot\mathbin{{\,||\,}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}}}\cdot_{j}^{\prime}
.

Then, the emitter set
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\kappa}}^{\prime\prime}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\kappa}}^{\prime\prime}=({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\kappa}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}{\kappa}}^{\prime})\cap\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{k}\mid k\in K\}
.

Note that by
Definition

34
, the acceptance transitions of
\cdot\mathbin{{\,||\,}}\cdot^{\prime}
consist of the acceptance transitions
of both
\cdot
and
\cdot^{\prime}
; common transitions are synchronised via the set
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}
, hence they are only enabled in a state if both
\cdot
and
\cdot^{\prime}
can take them. Further, an event type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
belongs to the emitter set of

\cdot\mathbin{{\,||\,}}\cdot^{\prime}
if and only if (
i
)
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
belongs to the
emitter set of either
\cdot
or
\cdot^{\prime}
, and (
ii
) either
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}
and both machines accept
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}
and one of the machines accepts
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
.
This means a machine either implements an interfacing role and thus only emits events in
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}
or it is non-interfacing and thus only emits events not in
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}T}
.
Also note that, as with swarm protocol composition (
Definition

2
), we do not introduce new syntax for composed machines: composed machines are treated as ordinary machines, to maintain backward compatibility with existing
tooling (that we extend in
Section

6
).

Example 35

(Relating swarm protocol composition, projection, and machine composition)
.

Consider the swarm protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
in
Figures

1
and
4
, and the subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
from
Example

22
. Also consider the projected machines
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}
in
Figure

11
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}{}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}?\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}?\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\mathbf{0}}}
. If we compose these two machines (using
Definition

34
), then we derive the machine denoted as
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}
in
Figure

11
, and therefore we have:

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}{}\,\mathbin{{\,||\,}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}{}\;\;=\;\;({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}})\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{D}}}

Note that this holds because, in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
, the roles of both projected machines subscribe to the interfacing event types that synchronise the composed swarm protocols (by
Definition

2
).
∎

5.2
Machine Adaptation

In
Definition

36
we introduce a function
\mathcal{A}

that
adapts
a single machine
\cdot
, projected from a swarm protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{k}
, so that it behaves correctly when used in a swarm
with other adapted machines that were projected from swarm protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
.
Intuitively, the adaptation function
\mathcal{A}
restructures the transition graph of
\cdot
in two steps (described in more detail below): (1) it adds automatically generated acceptance transitions to wait for extra synchronisation event types required by a composed subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
, and (2) it disables transitions whose continuations are incompatible with the other projections. Notably,
\mathcal{A}
does not rewrite the logic of
\cdot
:
the adapted machine looks different from
\cdot
(see
Example

37
below) but is still just the original automaton with a thin structural wrapper that delays progress until required synchronisation events are emitted by other machines, or disables incompatible paths.

Definition 36

(Machine adaptation for swarm composition)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
be swarm protocols with subscriptions

{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{n}
.
Let
\cdot
be a machine that, for some
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, realises role
\mathtt{R}
in the swarm protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{k}

for subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{k}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
be a well-formed subscription for
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}

(obtained
via
Algorithm

1
).
Then, we define the
machine adaptation
\mathcal{A}
as:

\mathcal{A}\!\left(\cdot,({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i})_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}},\mathtt{R},k,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}\right)\;=\;\left({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}\mathbin{{\,||\,}}\ldots\mathbin{{\,||\,}}\left(\cdot\mathbin{{\,||\,}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{k}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}\right)\mathbin{{\,||\,}}\ldots\mathbin{{\,||\,}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}\right).

We also set
{\mathcal{A}\!\left(\cdot,({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i})_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}},\mathtt{R},k,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}\right)}.\!\operatorname{UP}

as the set of branching, joining, or looping event types selected by
Algorithm

1
: this overapproximates the set of branching/joining/looping event types of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
.

To better see the effect of the adaptation
\mathcal{A}
in
Definition

36
, observe that by
Algorithm

1
we have
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{k}\subseteq\sigma
, hence the subscriptions
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{k}
may differ; consequently,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{k}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{k}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{k}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}
may not coincide.
Additionally, might be an interfacing role; therefore, it might appear in other protocols besides
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{k}
. Hence, the input machine
\cdot
must be adapted to play role while conforming to
all
protocols where appears.
Consequently, the adaption of
\cdot
involves the following two steps (that were outlined above):

1.

\cdot
is composed with
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{k}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}
to adapt it to the behaviour expected by the subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
(which may be larger than
\sigma_{k}
). This adaptation may force
\cdot
to await and accept additional events types, i.e., those in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}({\mathtt{}})
that are not in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{k}({\mathtt{}})
.

2.

\cdot
is also composed with the projections obtained from the remaining protocols of the composition. This step may prune some transitions of
\cdot
that are absent in the other protocols and add necessary transitions from the other protocols to ensure correct synchronisation in the composed swarm.

We extend the adaptation mechanism in
Definition

36
to whole swarms:
Algorithm

2
takes a set of swarms that respectively realise protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
, and automatically generates a swarm that realises
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
. Specifically,
Algorithm

2
uses
Algorithm

1
to find a suitable subscription for the composition, and then it applies the adaptation function in
Definition

36
. (For a more detailed version of
Algorithm

2
, see
Algorithm

3
in
Appendix

E
.)

Input:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
composable protocols, which are respectively realised by swarms
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{1},\ldots,{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{n}
for the subscriptions
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{n}
.

•

Mappings
\operatorname{r}_{1},\ldots,\operatorname{r}_{n}
that respectively map machines to the role they implement, i.e.,
\operatorname{r}_{k}\!\left({{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{k}(i)}\right)
is the role realised by the machine at
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{k}(i)
.

Output:

A swarm that realises
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
.

1:

Apply
Algorithm

1
to obtain a well-formed subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
.

2:

For each swarm
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{k}
, for each machine
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{k}(i)
: adapt
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{k}(i)
into
\cdot^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}\!\left(\cdot,({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i})_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}},\operatorname{r}_{k}\!\left({{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{k}(i)}\right),k,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}\right)
according to
Definition

36
.

3:

Return a swarm built up from the machines adapted in the previous step.

Algorithm 2

Swarm composition (shortened version of
Algorithm

3
in
Appendix

E
).

Example 37

(Composing swarms by adapting their machines)
.

Recall the swarm
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}
whose machines are projected from the swarm protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
in
Example

29
.

In order to compose
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}
with another swarm
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{F}}
projected from
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}

using
Algorithm

2
, we apply the adaptation in
Definition

36
to all machines in both
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{F}}
, and we obtain a swarm
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{F}}}
that realises the composed protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
.
We illustrate this on the forklift machine
{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{FL}}}
.
We first apply
Algorithm

1
(as in
Example

15
) to obtain a subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}^{\prime}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}^{\prime}
-well-formed, and which contains the initial
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
and the interfacing event types needed for synchronising the machines that implement
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
. By
Definition

36
, the machine adaptation of
{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}}
is:

\mathcal{A}\!\left({}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}},{\left({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}\right),\mathtt{FL}},1,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}^{\prime}\right)\;=\;\left({}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\mathbin{{\,||\,}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}^{\prime}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{FL}}}\right)\mathbin{{\,||\,}}{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}^{\prime}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{FL}}}

where “1” is the index of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
in the tuple
({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}})
.
The stages of the adaptation are depicted in
Figure

12
, where the state numbers show the relationship between the original machine and its adaptations. The original
{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}}
is adapted with the projection of its original swarm protocol (
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
) using
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}^{\prime}
(computed with
Algorithm

1
) to obtain the machine
{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}^{\prime}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{FL}}}
: observe that this adapted machine has an additional state 3 which forces the machine to wait for an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partOK}}
after
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{pos}}
, between the original states 2 and 4. When
{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}^{\prime}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{FL}}}
is further composed with
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}^{\prime}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{FL}}}
, the adapted machine loses the possibility in state 4 of awaiting another event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{partReq}}
and looping to state 2: hence, in state 4, the adapted machine can only await and process an event of type
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{closingTime}}
.
Using
Algorithm

2
, this final machine is added to the adapted swarm
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{F}}}
.
Then,
Algorithm

2
repeats this adaptation for every machine in
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{W}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{F}}}
.
∎

:
How
Definition

36
adapts the machine
{}_{\mathtt{FL}}\cdot{}_{\mathtt{FL}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{FL}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
.

We can now state the correctness of our swarm composition procedure.
(Proof in
Appendix

F
.)

Theorem 38

(Correctness of swarm adaptation and composition)
.

Let be a swarm obtained by applying
Algorithm

2
to swarms
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{1},\ldots,{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{n}
, protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
and
subscriptions
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{n}
. Then, is eventually faithful to
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
.

The key insight in the proof of
Theorem

38
is that the subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
computed by
Algorithm

1
includes all the
events needed for the synchronization of the composed swarm. This ensures that each
adapted machine is a correct projection of the composed swarm protocol. Therefore, a machine adapted from the input
swarm
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{i}
will execute without desynchronising and diverging from other machines adapted from another input swarm
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}_{j}
(for
i,j\in\{1\dots n\}
such that
i\neq j
).

6
Implementation and Experiments

We now present our implementation of the theory in
Sections

3
,
4
and
5
.
In
Section

6.1

we discuss how we extend the open source Actyx toolkit
[
4
]
to support branch-tracking machine semantics
and machine adaptation (which are key elements of our compositional approach),
and to statically verify our new, compositional well-formedness of swarm protocols.
Then, in
Section

6.2
we benchmark different strategies for
computing the subscriptions of composed swarms.
Our implementation is availble in the companion artifact of this paper
[
26
]
.

6.1
Branch Tracking, Adaptations, and New Verification Facilities

Branch Tracking and
Adaptation.

The
machine-runner
library
[
3
]
of the Actyx
toolkit features a TypeScript API to program
machine
implementations
.
For instance,
Figure

3
yields the machine implementation
of a machine
\cdot
(defined according to

Definition

17
) for the role
\mathtt{D}
of our

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
use case.

The
machine-runner
library also helps instantiating and running machine implementations in a
swarm using the Actyx middleware
[
1
]
which
implements the log propagation mechanisms described
in
Section

4.4
.
Let us discuss our extension of
machine-runner
.

In order to support our new branch-tracking semantics
(
Definition

24
),
(1)

we added a pointer
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}.\mathsf{last_{up}}
(“previous updating event”)
to each event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}
, and
(2)
modified
machine-runner

to produce and access
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}e}.\mathsf{last_{up}}
.
We also added new methods that automatically adapt machine implementations according to

Definition

36
.
This allows developers to re-use and deploy existing machine implementations in composed
swarms.
For instance, to adapt the machine implementation

door
of role

\mathtt{D}
in
Figure

3
to the composed swarm

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Factory}}
, a programmer can simply invoke:

adaptedDoor

=

adaptMachine
('
D
',

listOfProtocols
,

0,

subscriptions
,

[
door
,

s0
])

The parameters to
adaptMachine
are the machine role
\mathtt{D}
, a list of the swarm protocols in the composition, the index of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{Warehouse}}
in that list, a well-formed (and automatically generated) subscription for the composition, and the original
door
machine with its initial state.

New Static Verification Facilities.

The Actyx toolkit can statically verify several properties through the

machine-check

[
2
]
library.
More precisely,
machine-check
can verify (
i
) the
well-formedness (as defined in
[
41
]
) of a
protocol under a given subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
and (
ii
)
whether the machine implementation of a role
\mathtt{R}
of
emits and accepts events as specified by
the projection of
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\mathbin{\,\downarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}}_{\mathtt{\mathtt{R}}}
.

We extended
machine-check
to verify our new well-formedness
of protocol compositions (
Definitions

2
and
12
),
and generate subscriptions for which a composition is well-formed
(
Algorithm

1
).
We also modified the
machine-check
projection code (which was based on
[
41
]
)
to conform to our notion of projections in
Definition

21
.
This enables (1) the static verification of machine implementations in composed swarm protocols,
and (2) the automatic adaptation of previously-implemented machines to work correctly in a composed swarm.

6.2
Experiments on Compositional Subscription Computation

A key element for the correct composition of protocols and swarms is
the availability of a subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
suitable for composition
(required by
Definition

31
and

Theorem

32
).
We analyse the performance and trade-offs of

Algorithm

1
, which is instrumental for
computing subscriptions suitable for swarm composition
(cf.
Section

5
).

Algorithm

1
may compute a subscription larger than necessary,
which may lead to non-optimal performance in the deployed swarm.
Therefore, we compare
Algorithm

1
with a naive
algorithm (available in
Appendix

G
) that computes the “exact” subscription by directly
applying
Definition

12
.
More precisely, this algorithm accepts a set
\{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}\}

of composable protocols and a set of subscriptions
\{{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{n}\}
,
expands the composed protocol according to

Definition

2

and applies
Definition

12
to produce the smallest
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}\supseteq{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}_{n}

for which
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
-well-formed.
As mentioned in
Section

3.3
,
the expansion of the composed protocol makes the time complexity of
this algorithm exponential in the number of input protocols.

Benchmark Selection and Experimental Setup.

We created a benchmark consisting of 454 sets of randomly-generated swarm protocols.
Each set consists of
n
swarm protocols

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
(with
n\in\{1,\ldots,10\}
) with up to 9
roles each; each role emits up to 9 event types.
In order to cover a broad range of possible practical cases, the
random generation produces variety of branching and looping patterns.
Moreover, protocols
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i+1}
share an
interfacing role, for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
.
All randomly-generated protocols follow a pattern: two interfacing event types
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{2}
may be separated by
a random number of choices with non-interfacing event types, but

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{2}
are always enabled in the same order in all
protocols they occur in.

The experiments were conducted by invoking both
Algorithm

1
and our naive “exact” algorithm on empty
input subscriptions: this is to compute the smallest possible output subscription for each protocol composition
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
.
The execution times were measured using Criterion
[
24
]
; for each randomly-generated set of swarm protocols, Criterion invoked
Algorithm

1
and the “exact” algorithm at least 50 times
after 3 seconds of warm-up, reporting averages, medians, and standard deviations (including outliers).

Results.

Figure

13
plots the execution time measured in our
experiments against the number of transitions in the composition
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
;
note that the number of transitions grows exponentially in
n
.
As expected,
Algorithm

1
is faster and more
scalable than the “exact” algorithm, which suffers from the exponential
blow-up of the composition.
For comparison, the generation of a subscription for a composition of
protocols with
{\sim}10^{5}
transitions takes
{\sim}0.01
seconds
using
Algorithm

1
and
{\sim}10
seconds
using the “exact” algorithm.
Notably, the trend shown in
Figure

13
persists even if we discount the time
needed to expand the composition of the input protocols before invoking the “exact” algorithm:
this is because the “exact” algorithm spends more time applying
Definition

12
to generate
a well-formed subscription for the expanded composition.

E_{\textit{frac}}

Figure 15
:
Comparison of subscription sizes obtained using our definition of well-formedness and that of
[
41
]
.

Figure

14
compares the accuracy of the two algorithms,
measured as the fraction of event types involved in the generated
subscription
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
.
Specifically,
E_{\textit{frac}}
is the fraction of all event types
subscribed to by a role in
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}
on average across all
roles.
For instance,
E_{\textit{frac}}=0.2
means that, on average, a role
subscribes to one out of five event types, while

E_{\textit{frac}}=1.0
means that all roles subscribe to every event
type.
In general, a lower
E_{\textit{frac}}
means less subscriptions and a
higher degree of concurrency, leading to a swarm with smaller and more
efficient machines.
The plot shows that
the “exact” algorithm requires roles to subscribe to
{\sim}22.4\%

of all event types in the input protocols.

Algorithm

1
requires subscribing to

{\sim}29.9\%
of the event types, which is quite close to the
“exact” algorithm.
The difference occurs because
Algorithm

1
may not see that parts of the input protocols
become unreachable in the composition, and thus, may produce redundant subscriptions to some updating event types; also,

Algorithm

1
adds interfacing event types to subscriptions (even when not required by well-formedness). This is necessary to ensure correct machine adaptation without expanding the composed protocol.

Comparing Subscription Sizes with Those of
[
41
]
.

In
Section

2.2
we mentioned that the well-formedness definition of
[
41
]
may lead to larger subscriptions than necessary, especially compared to our
Definition

12
and
Algorithm

1
.
We now quantify this claim experimentally.
Figure

15
compares the sizes of minimal well-formed subscriptions obtained using our definition of well-formedness (
Definition

12
) and that of
[
41
]

for 2093 randomly generated swarm protocols. Since
[
41
]
does not support concurrent events, these 2093 protocols contain no compositions and no concurrency.
As a consequence, the minimal subscriptions that ensure well-formedness (for both
[
41
]
and our
Definition

12
) are expected to be larger than those in
Figure

14
.
The plot in
Figure

15
shows that our
Definition

12
and
Algorithm

1
require roles to subscribe to
{\sim}47.6\%
of all event types in the input protocols on average,
whereas
[
41
]
requires
{\sim}70.8\%
.
10
10

10

In
Figure

15
, the “exact” and
Alg. 1
boxplot both contain two outliers with
E_{\textit{frac}}=1
, greater than all values in the boxplot for
[
41
]
. These are two very small protocols with a choice where a role
\mathtt{R}
only appears in one of the branches.
In this case, our
Definition

12
requires
\mathtt{R}
to subscribe to event types in all branches of the choice, whereas
[
41
]
only requires
\mathtt{R}
to subscribe to the branch where
\mathtt{R}
participates. As a result, our
Definition

12
requires additional subscriptions for such cases – but it also provides a stronger guarantee w.r.t.
[
41
]
: a machine playing role
\mathtt{R}
can determine if the swarm is following a branch of the swarm protocol where
\mathtt{R}
is not involved.

Figure

15
also shows that, for non-concurrent protocols,
Algorithm

1
yields the same output subscriptions as the “exact” algorithm: this is because the protocols contain no interfacing event types, nor do they exhibit behaviour restrictions
like those in
Example

4
.

7
Related Work

This work embraces the research path taken in
[
41
]
,
advocating

local-first cooperation

[
39
,
38
]
in the design,
analysis, and development of distributed applications modelled as swarms of machines,
using swarm protocols as a specification language.
We tackle the problem of compositional design and verification of swarms –
which was left open in
[
41
]
and, to the best of our knowledge, is not
addressed elsewhere.
The importance of modularisation for managing software complexity has been well
established since early work
[
45
]
:
separate parts of a system should be composed via well-defined

interfaces
that hide implementation details between components.
We accomplish this using
interfacing roles
(
Definition

1
)
whose emitted events act as the glue that binds different swarm
protocols and swarms into larger ones.

Technical Differences w.r.t.
[
41
]
.

Besides introducing our new compositionality results, in this work we simplify the theory of
[
41
]

aligning it to the common usages of the open source Actyx toolkit
[
4
]
,
without reducing the expressiveness of the model:

•

The grammar in
Definition

17
is as
in
[
41
]
, except that we do not explicitly model the
“commands” a machine performs when emitting events:

we assume a 1:1 correspondence between events and commands, leaving the
latter implicit. Moreover, our transitions range over single event types
instead of finite non-empty sequences of event types.

•

In
Definition

27
, our rule

Local
simplifies the corresponding rule
in
[
41
]
: since we have a 1:1
correspondence between events and commands, the shuffling operator
of
[
41
]
is unnecessary.
Also, our rule
Global
simplifies the corresponding rule
in
[
41
]
.
11
11

11

More specifically, the closure property imposed on the sub-log
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{}}^{\prime}

in
[
41
]
is not necessary in this work. This
is because we do not need to ensure that for each event in the global log emitted by a machine of a swarm
, say
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(j)
, includes each event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathit{{}}}^{\prime}
that
precedes in the local log of
{{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}}(j)
.

A key challenge of our compositional approach, compared to the
“monolithic” approach of
[
41
]
, is that in the monolithic setting, the eventual fidelity of a machine
\cdot
is only ensured if
\cdot
knows and accepts the
guard event
for every command that precedes
\cdot
’s event emissions – and this, in our one-event-per-command setting, would mean that
\cdot
must know and accept all events that precede
\cdot
’s emissions. However, in our composed swarms, a machine
\cdot
projected from one swarm protocol may encounter events produced by other machines and protocols that
\cdot
did not originally anticipate.
To address this, our revised machine semantics (
Section

4.3
)
tracks event causality more precisely, requiring a machine
\cdot
that plays role
\mathtt{R}
to accept only those events that can affect
\mathtt{R}
’s behaviour.
This is achieved via
updating event types
and our improved swarm semantics (
Definition

27
): each event carries minimal causal information, i.e., a pointer to the last relevant updating event observed by the emitting machine.
This allows a machine to decide whether to accept or ignore an event.
The main result of
[
41
]
is that any swarm
that realises a well-formed is eventually faithful to ; we generalise this result to composed swarms and protocols in

Sections

4.5
and
5.2
.

Global Types and Behavioural Types.

Swarm protocols are inspired by
global types
, a.k.a.
choreographies

[
33
,
34
]
; they
describe multiparty interaction pattern from a global point of view,
and they can be projected onto roles to obtain local
specifications, against which one can check the correctness of
implementations.
The literature on global types includes compositional approaches: e.g.,
partial choreographies that can be composed while preserving deadlock-freedom
[
43
]
,
and choreography refinement to replace part of a choreography with a more complex one
[
21
]
. However, the highly dynamic and non-deterministic semantics of swarms is
significantly different from the semantics in global types literature. When compared to the literature on behavioural
types
[
27
,
35
,
6
]
, our approach
and
[
41
]
depart from the usual assumptions in several
key ways.
Standard behavioural typing approaches ensure all interacting components are aware of the current state of the distributed computation.
In contrast, we allow inconsistencies between machines to emerge, as long as consistency is
eventually
achieved
[
15
]
:
e.g., machines can make transient discordant choices that are reconciled as the logs propagate in the swarm.
This is akin to data replication methods, where there is a trade-off between availability, consistency, and partition tolerance
[
29
]
. Several approaches have emerged to balance this trade-off, including conflict-free replicated data types
[
48
]
, cloud types
[
16
]
, consistency contracts
[
50
]
, invariants
[
31
,
37
,
8
]
, linearizability
[
52
]
, and operational models for applications such as GSP
[
17
,
30
]
;
a key difference in our approach lies in how we achieve eventual consistency, and how we introduce compositional mechanisms.
Moreover, in standard behavioural type sytems, the variability in the number of participants has been addressed via
parametricity
, e.g. in multiparty session types
[
53
,
22
,
23
,
18
,
36
]
:
this requires explicit handling of
the parameters of the protocol.
Instead, swarm protocols do not restrict the
number of instances playing a given role.

In behavioural types literature, most compositional approaches require
component protocols to be aware that they are part of a composition: e.g. hybrid types
[
28
]
distinguish
inter-component interactions from intra-component interactions; other
approaches allow composition via open endpoints
[
43
,
51
]
.
An approach closer to ours is
Participants-as-Interfaces

(PaI)
[
10
,
12
,
13
,
11
,
14
]
, where interfaces are defined by
roles that perform complementary behaviours. Like us, PaI allows viewing
a closed system as an open one – but PaI uses complementary roles as
forwarders
to connect protocols, whereas our interfacing roles simply play their roles and do not act as forwarders.

Choreographic Programming Languages
[
42
]

differ from swarm protocols in both goals and approach. Choreographic programs provide a global
implementation
of communicating roles via point-to-point messages, with notions of compositionality inspired by programming languages theory (e.g., higher-order choreographic programs
[
49
,
20
]
). Swarm protocols and projected machines, by contrast, serve as specifications only: machines are implemented and checked separately, e.g., using the Actyx toolkit. Moreover, machines operate under asynchronous, subscription-based event propagation, with possible event reordering and machine replication. These goals and semantics are significantly different from choreographic programming languages, and thus, they lead to a different notion of composition.

8
Conclusion and Future Work

We have presented novel results on the compositional verification and implementation of swarms and swarm protocols. Our results enable the compositional verification of swarm systems, as well as the reuse of the implementations of swarm machines in larger composed systems.
This enables a compositional swarm design process, as well as the seamless extension of existing swarm systems with new functionality.
We believe our novel compositionality results can be applied to other local-first distributed systems.
We have implemented our results in a custom version of the open source Actyx toolkit for swarm application development
[
4
]
, thus enabling the compositional design and implementation of swarms in practice.
We have also streamlined the swarm protocol theory presented in
[
41
]
, while aligning it with the observed use of Actyx tools in the real world.

Future Work.

Swarms and machines have some similarity with
actor systems

[
32
,
5
]
(asynchronous communication, replication)
but differ significantly in their semantics (out-of-order event propagation). We are not aware of specific work on the composition of actor systems – although the communicating finite-state machines used in
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
have analogies with actors (message buffers can be seen as a form of mailbox), hence their approach to compositionality may be applicable to actors. This is a valuable area of study, and we will investigate whether our compositionality results can be applied to actor systems.

Currently, our method of composing protocols and swarms (
Algorithms

1
and
2
)
and our correctness result (
Theorem

38
) are limited to compositions of
sequential
protocols without internal concurrency: i.e., we support compositions of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
where each
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
is a sequential protocol (by
Definition

14
) – and this allows us to
reuse and compose all swarm protocols that are well-formed according to
[
41
]
, and all machines projected from such protocols. However, we do not currently support arbitrary compositions of concurrent protocols, like e.g.
({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{2})\,||\,({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{3}\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{4})
. To address this limitation, we plan to build upon our results
and generalise them to design a full-fledged

module system for swarm development
.
We will also study better compositional methods for the computation of subscriptions and the minimisation of swarm machines,
in order to improve the precision of our
Algorithm

1
(measured in
Section

6.2
)
without losing scalability for larger swarms.

Appendices

Appendix A
Proof of
Theorem

16

Algorithm

1
makes use of a key insight: the function
subscribers
overapproximates
roles
.
We now outline the reasoning behind this insight and formalise it below in
Lemma

39
. Suppose
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{roles}(,,)
. By
Definition

10
there is a path

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{0}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l_{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l_{n}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{0}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i+1}
are not concurrent for all
i<n
.
Whenever two consecutive (and thus non-concurrent) event types
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i+1}
along the path above belong to different input swarm protocols, the corresponding path segment
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}_{i+1}
must include a sequence of interfacing event types
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{m}
that enforce their order:
i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}_{i+1}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}+1}
.
We take the sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n-1}
and, for each such case, replace
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i+1}
with the sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
. This yields a new sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}_{n^{\prime\prime}}
where adjacent events from different protocols are guaranteed to be interfacing.
If we now discard any non-interfacing event type from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime}_{n^{\prime\prime}}
, we obtain a sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{n^{\prime\prime\prime}}
consisting solely of interfacing event types.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
, by iteratively applying the
Interfacing
rule we get
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{n^{\prime\prime\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{n^{\prime\prime\prime}-1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
,
\ldots
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
.
Since the first event
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}
belong to the same protocol, say
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
, we have
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{subscribers}({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i},)
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}
.
Therefore we conclude that whenever
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{roles}(,,)
, then
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{subscribers}({\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i},)
(for some
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}
).

We express this as a technical lemma:

Lemma 39
.

We apply
Algorithm

1
. Assume
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime},)
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{n}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{1}\,||\,\ldots\,||\,{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{n}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}_{i}
for
i\leq n
.
It holds
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{subscribers}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i})
for any
i\leq n
with
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}}
.

Proof of
Lemma

39
.

Since
\mathtt{R}\in\mathit{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}},{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime},)
,
there is a path
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0,0}\mathsf{}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{0}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l_{1}}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l_{n}}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}}
in the composition such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{0}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.4,0.3}\sigma}(\mathtt{R})
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i+1}
are not concurrent for
i<n
.

We now show that we can assume WLOG that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n-1}
are interfacing event types such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i},\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i+1}
occur in the same swarm protocol for
i<n
.
Assume, towards contradiction, that this is not the case. This means that there are
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{i+1}
that do not occur in the same swarm protocols and they are not both interfacing. Further, there are no event types in
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{l}}_{i+1}
that can be added to the sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.2}\mathtt{t}}_{n-1}
